in writing. taking account of the scientifically substantiated characteristics of climate change is essential for making decisions in this area. the risk of making wrong decisions based on false conclusions is at least as great as the risk of inaction. one thing is certain: climate change is a scientific fact, and it requires quick and effective solutions. a calm consideration of scientific facts can also help us to assess how market-based environmental instruments can contribute to improving environmental quality. we believe that further efforts are needed by member states to promote market-friendly incentives. the creation of an emissions trading system, which takes a market-based approach to reducing emissions, is a welcome development. the operation of the system is evidence that market, competition and environmental protection are not mutually exclusive. in hungary, there are many examples of how wrong conclusions can lead to wrong decisions. biomass farming is really about burning wood, and the harmful consequences of the forced subsidisation of biofuels are becoming increasingly apparent. the measures taken in these two areas do not meet the sustainability requirements and do not provide a market-friendly solution to the problem. we would like to emphasise that climate change requires measures that are not only scientifically sound but also take account of market mechanisms.

in writing. - in the case of public money, such as agricultural aid, it is necessary to ensure that it is distributed fairly on the basis of objective criteria. we therefore agree with the need for surveys to be carried out to ascertain these conditions on farms. the application of these principles must not, however, become another source of bureaucratic burden for farmers, especially small and mediumsized farmers, whose resources are very limited or non-existent. it cannot be a means of preventing farmers from obtaining the support to which they are entitled, because of technical or other shortcomings which are not their own, as has sometimes happened in portugal with satellite recognition and identification. we therefore welcome the fact that the report acknowledges the significant methodological and technical difficulties in many member states and stresses the need for the commission to provide them with the necessary technical assistance and advice on the recognition of agricultural holdings by satellite. we also want to emphasize once again the need for the member states' authorities to ensure that the data collected in the context of satellite reconnaissance is accessed and used exclusively for the intended purposes.

in writing. – the report reflects correctly our opinions on the evolution of the cfsp in the future. the cfsp is a crucially important element in the eu’s foreign policy. i would like to welcome the improvements brought by the lisbon treaty regarding external actions. its implementation would make the cfsp more efficient and more coherent. the new posts created by the treaty could give a greater visibility to the eu, but i believe it is essential to define the different roles in order to ensure that their different functions contribute to the coherence and efficiency of the cfsp. if we want the eu to be an active force for international peace and stability, we must have all the necessary tools and instruments at our disposal; but to achieve this, more political will from the member states is needed. the eu presence in the globe is widening, and we can find eu missions all over the world. by helping to build security and stability in the wider world, the eu is also helping to make life safer for her own people. this is the best way to defend its security and promote its values. in my opinion these are steps in the right direction.
in writing. the eu-us summit in a few days' time is a good opportunity to review the development of transatlantic relations in key areas. we believe that energy cooperation should be placed high on the agenda. energy security remains a priority on both sides of the atlantic. it is well known that the bulk of the stocks are located in countries which do not always meet democratic requirements. therefore, joint action and further efforts are needed to promote human rights and the rule of law. it is important to emphasise that the spread of democratic principles also means greater security of energy supply. in addition to the above, future cooperation should focus increasingly on the fight against climate change. it is a welcome development that the united states has committed itself to laying the foundations for a post-kyoto regime. the eu member states and the united states should take the lead in ensuring that the most developed countries agree on the main pillars of the international treaty before the end of the year. the recent package of emissions reduction proposals in the us congress is yet another sign that washington wants to take an active role in the fight against climate change. oil prices at previously unimaginable levels are urgently calling for concerted international action on energy policy. we are convinced that the responsibility of the united states and europe in this area is inescapable.
in writing. – eu-us relations in many issues have been through substantial changes over the last years and are moving into a phase of consolidation for many items of cooperation. the recent case of the negotiations on lifting visa restrictions for travellers from the eu is an excellent example – in spite of the positive outcome – showing why, in order to ensure successful talks between the us and the eu, we ought to avoid mere bilateral agreements which actually undermine the negotiating powers of the eu as a whole. having a package agreement encompassing the 27 member states, based on the distribution of competences between the union and the member states, was indeed crucial in order to support the eu visa common policy. it is essential to ensure that, where community competences apply, we secure eu-wide deals with our strategic partners such as the united states. in my opinion, it is in the interest of both partners. it is indeed the only way to ensure that the visa waiver programme brings full reciprocal visa free travel and equal treatment for all our citizens as regards the status of their passports, just as for us citizens.
in the midst of the worldwide crisis over illegal logging, a landmark law was passed in the united states on may 15. congress created the first law in the world to ban the importation of illegally harvested timber. in 2006, 10% of us imports were from illegally harvested timber. after the law was passed, the analysis highlighted that this law will help american manufacturers compete, protect jobs, and that it is a clear message that will be heard by forest communities. how long and what is the european union waiting for, i ask? it's a shame and a disgrace to wait with our hands folded. but there is something we can do as members of parliament: i suggest that everyone sign petition no 23, which was tabled a few months ago and whose aim is precisely to persuade the commission to finally follow the united states in introducing legislation while we still have forests. thank you very much.
turkish prime minister erdogan gave a highly controversial speech in cologne in february this year, calling assimilation a crime against humanity. on the basis of the free choice of identity, this statement is unacceptable, the preservation of cultural diversity is an important goal, but the individual cannot be deprived of the choice. for western european turks, dual identity may be the natural state. erdogan's demand for a university in germany with turkish as its mother tongue is unrealistic, because in several eu countries even the indigenous national minorities who have lived there for centuries do not have their own state university, like the hungarians in transylvania, where the hungarian community was deprived of an independent state university under the communist dictatorship. it is important that turkey does not manipulate the migrant minorities in western europe for domestic political purposes. it would also be nice if prime minister erdogan swept around his own house: the situation of the kurds, the recognition of the genocide against the armenians and the equality of women.
in writing. the union has a responsibility to prevent sexual exploitation and trafficking in human beings at sporting events held on its territory. the 2008 european football championship is a major sporting event that brings together a large number of people, which temporarily increases the demand for sexual services. it is therefore essential to defend ourselves with transitional campaigns, such as those initiated by ms. zaborska during the 2006 fifa world cup and which she argues should continue this time. however, i believe that in order to reach as many people as possible, the initiative would need to be widely publicised in the media (including on the ec's official website) by political and sports leaders or by athletes and fans (as in the 'youth voices against racism' programme). but a transitional campaign is not enough. the commission has also adopted a communication on the implementation of the eu's action plan against trafficking in human beings. i would like to draw the commission's attention to the fact that many countries are experiencing shortcomings in the transposition and interpretation of directives, which is causing delays in their implementation. i therefore consider it of the utmost importance that we get an accurate answer to the question of the zborskaya commission concerning directive 2004/81/ec. although border control plays an important role in screening for sexual exploitation and trafficking in human beings at sporting events in the union, i believe that this cannot be achieved by tightening internal border controls but by strengthening external ones, by ensuring adequate cooperation between border guards. we're not making it harder for law-abiding citizens to participate in european events.
in writing. - my fellow members of parliament! i would like to draw attention once again to the vulnerability of roma women in the area of trafficking and forced prostitution, following the question of president zaborská. social exclusion and the frequent lack of official documents make roma girls particularly vulnerable and easy targets for trafficking profiteers. their situation is further exacerbated by racial and gender prejudice both in society and in their own communities, and by the general lack of trust of roma in the justice system. specific programmes are needed to help victims of trafficking and their families and to prevent adolescent girls from being forced to live and work in exploitative environments. it is also essential to establish advisory networks and centres, both by civil society organisations and by member states, and to collect and analyse reliable statistical data on the relationship between roma communities and trafficking in human beings. in the latter area, while some civil and international organisations are able to show some results, a substantially greater involvement of both the european commission and the member states is needed. however, the main driver of trafficking and sexual exploitation is the deprivation of human life in the daily struggle for survival, where the lack of economic and educational opportunities significantly increases the risk of illegal activities. therefore, in addition to awareness programs and assistance to those already victimized, the elimination of this inhuman misery is our joint priority.
on behalf of the ppe-de group. thank you very much, mr. president. health and the consumption of quality food are of paramount importance to european citizens in the 21st century. it is our job as representatives to ensure that, and that requires consumer protection legislation, procedures like haccp. but it's equally important not to overdo it, not to over-regulate our lives, because that's not about quality of life, that's about bureaucracy. the present compromise proposal is, in my view, a step in the right direction. experience has shown that the haccp requirements currently in force are in many cases unduly restrictive. moreover, they are not always applicable in the hospitality and hotel industries, because they assume food industry conditions, working methods. nevertheless, the new member states, including hungary, have met or exceeded the haccp requirements. and that's what our businesses have been doing, and many of them have been doing it for centuries, and that's not what we're trying to do, and that's why we have to change it. first of all, it is important to emphasise that restaurants are not food factories, so they should not be required to meet the same standards, because this is at the expense of quality and traditions. let's not forget he had a michelin star before haccp. we need to reduce the overwhelming administrative burden. i support the idea that the haccp exemption should only apply to micro, small and medium-sized enterprises that are able to carry out food hygiene controls. i regret that while the taxation of micro enterprises is covered, the exemption of small and medium-sized enterprises is only covered in the text. it's important to be clear about this, to say it. finally, the system must be more flexible than the main one. the competent national authority shall decide what is common sense in a given case. i congratulate the reporter on her report.

in writing. the official inspections due in hungary in the summer show that there are often hygiene problems with the occasional vendors, bakeries and market stalls. these phenomena are known in other countries. i agree that we need to cut red tape, especially when regulations make it impossible for small businesses to do their jobs. but reducing the scope of legislation cannot be at the expense of food safety. europe has a unique and exceptionally good food safety regulation compared to other continents. there may be shortcomings in its implementation, but these problems need not necessarily be addressed by deregulation. therefore, we can only exempt from haccp, eu hygiene control, in cases where we can be absolutely sure that we are not causing harm. as the hungarian says, it's better to be afraid than scared.

madam president, in europe we are currently recycling only 27% of our waste and almost half of our waste ends up in landfills. this shows that we have to fundamentally change our waste management. most importantly we should encourage waste prevention, strengthen reuse and recycling and minimise the amount of landfill waste. therefore i welcome the fact that the waste hierarchy remained in the new compromise text after some debate. as regards prevention – the main aim of the legislation – i miss the waste stabilisation targets in the latest text, but we had already adopted them in the first reading. in the old member states one person generates almost twice as much household waste – 570 kg per year – as a person in the new member states – 300 to 350 kg per year. so the rich nations should start to decrease waste production first. the amount of generated waste is increasing on a european scale. therefore prevention programmes, as proposed in the compromise, are not enough: we must set binding targets to halt the growing waste generation. that is why i consider supporting amendment 48 which reintroduces the waste stabilisation target. i welcome the mandatory waste reuse and recycling targets, but i am afraid that the new text, which says that member states ‘shall take the necessary measures’ to achieve the targets is not enough. we need concrete, enforceable and binding targets, both for household and industrial waste. therefore i propose to support amendment 82, to ensure the enforcement of the recycling targets. having made these comments, i welcome the report and the work of mrs jackson, my friend guido sacconi and others.
thank you very much, madam president. i welcome the compromise package, but i must stress that this compromise is very fragile. it is therefore particularly important that each member state behaves responsibly and does not seek out any loopholes in the legislation. at present, the selective collection rate for municipal waste in hungary is 2%. i think there is nothing to add to this but to hope that the directive will finally move us away from this low level. in recent years, the number of waste management investments in the eastern half of europe has increased, mainly due to the support received from ispa and the cohesion funds. the main landfill sites were built and selective collection programmes were initiated in several municipalities, and in some places measures were taken to collect organic waste separately. there were no real waste prevention measures or investments and there is still no visible processing industry. it is therefore very important that the union sets the course, including for hungary. statistics show that virtually any community can achieve a 50% waste reduction by implementing dry recycling and home-based separate collection of organic waste. however, when imposing new obligations, we cannot ignore their feasibility and their costs. for example, can additional investments be built on the ispa projects? can the original contracts be amended? if not, then despite the need to increase separate collection, despite the legislation to reduce the amount of waste landfilled, the contracts concluded for 20 years require that the waste collected be landfilled in the built-up landfill. the adoption of this directive should not delay the possibility of making amendments to existing contracts. i congratulate the performer on his excellent work. thank you very much.
madam president, i have to ask you... the waste directive in its present form unfortunately threatens the spread of biogas use. i fully agree with my colleague mr schnellhardt of the european people's party that this directive is not suitable for biogas production, either in terms of the use of slurry or municipal waste. the waste directive in its present form unfortunately threatens the spread of biogas use and the jackson report is therefore very questionable. the definition of animal manure used in biogas production as waste in the waste directive is not clear. if it were to be covered by the directive, it would make it impossible to produce biogas from animal manure, which has significant energy, environmental and climate protection benefits. this legal inconsistency needs to be clarified as soon as possible and the commission's position on the biogas directive should therefore be adopted.
thank you, mr president. most of europe's rivers are shared by several countries, sharing both the benefits and the responsibilities of rivers. one member country gets the same quality of water as the previous one. for example, the danube flows through seven countries, the drau through six, and the rhine and the tisza through five. this is a major challenge and requires a high level of cooperation. hopefully, the adoption of the legislation will also end the seven-year-old foaming of the river rabah, because the only wild water river in hungary is currently only distributed to you and it is advisable to be painted in bathing suits that can be seen on leaflets and posters. rubber is just a litmus test, of course, that something is wrong with the current regulations, because if everyone is following all the limits, and there's still a thick foam on the river, then the regulations are not good. that's why we need to change now, and that's why i'm asking everyone to vote for the compromise package tomorrow. thank you very much.
my colleagues have already told me that fresh water is perhaps the most valuable resource of the 21st century, and i have to agree with that, with that idea. it is also true that we are doing well in europe, both in terms of quantity and quality of freshwater. i want to bring to your attention a question that has not been addressed before, and that is the fact that with climate change, precipitation is becoming more extreme, and with it, the distribution curve of freshwater availability, which means long, hot, dry summers and flood-like rainfall across europe. therefore, in addition to all the environmental care mentioned above, there is a need for water management that, unlike the current system, basically captures water and allows it to be stored for the dry season. and it would be very important to have this become a european community policy, so that we can preserve the quantity of fresh water, and the quality is ensured by this legislation. thank you, sir.
as mentioned in the previous item, a significant proportion of europe's natural disasters are water-related: droughts, floods, forest fires. one of the roots of the problem is in traditional flood control, where if there's a flood, we're interested in draining the water as soon as possible, and a few months later, we're surprised at how important it would have been to properly store rainfall during floods, extreme rainfall, in the event of drought or forest fires. so it would be very important, and in a sense, it would be preventive, if we could change our entire water management, change it to the idea that every drop of fresh water is a treasure that needs to be properly preserved for more difficult, drier times. and it would be very important, especially in the new member states, that this should become a community policy, and that we can build this up as a community policy from common resources, and in this way mitigate the damage of floods, droughts, forest fires.
in writing. poverty is a complex, relative and multidimensional problem, which raises economic, social and cultural issues. yet we must act against it, because in 2006 16% of the total population of the eu25 (78 million) lived below the poverty line! when the lisbon strategy was launched in march 2000, the european council called on the member states and the commission to act in order to achieve a significant reduction in poverty by 2010. having a measure of poverty is an important step, because it's important to recognize the problem in order to address it. i welcome the fact that 2010 has been designated as the european year for combating poverty and social exclusion. i hope that the year will contribute to the exchange of good practices and to the launching of new initiatives in all areas, in particular poverty prevention, monitoring, financial mechanisms and the fight against child poverty. it should be noted that surveys show that women are much more at risk of poverty than men and this is particularly true for women who are cumulatively disadvantaged. it is essential that the fight against poverty be based on the principle of equal treatment, so that the european year will also be marked by equal treatment. genuine equality of opportunity between men and women is a fundamental prerequisite for reducing the risk of poverty, as women are less likely to fall victim to poverty if they have a decent and well-paid job that they can reconcile with family responsibilities.
the rapporteur. - thank you very much, mr. president. officer, please. my fellow officers. there are regions and people in those regions who are not able to take advantage of the opportunities that we, with european funds, are trying to provide them, so that they can catch up with the eu average as quickly as possible, so that they can end the poverty in which they live. i have initiated this report to examine the reasons why we are not able to achieve progress in these areas and to make recommendations to reverse this process, to stop it. as the european commission's fourth cohesion report shows, cohesion policy is working very effectively at country level. in practice, in those countries that have already joined, that are receiving cohesion support, more progress has been made, a good track of catch-up has been started. however, if you look at it a little bit further down, the differences between the regions don't diminish that much. i would like to take hungary as an example, where the central region has reached 110% of the average gdp per capita in the european union, while in hungary there are four regions out of seven with a gdp per capita below 45%. but at least here, those differences don't grow. however, as this fourth cohesion report points out, the disparities between regions are in many cases continuing to grow. why is it that some regions are able to take advantage of the opportunities and others are not? if we look a little bit, we find very serious structural reasons behind this, spatial structural reasons. in these areas, where there is little development, there is clearly a lack of human capacity to bring about the right projects, a lack of basic infrastructure to bring investment to these areas, a lack of proper education, and a lack of proper health care. there are several regions where male life expectancy is more than 15 years lower than the national average, such as hungary. what are we supposed to do? since this is a very complex problem and it is clearly focused on a few small areas, small areas, i believe that we need to examine whether it is right to look at cohesion policy only at the regional level, at the level of the regions, or whether it is worthwhile to examine whether in some small areas where the biggest problems are, there is a need to take more focused, more targeted measures. so the first thing we need to do is to look at what statistics we can use to make decisions. when i wrote the report, i was aware of the problem, but i cannot give you a specific statistical comparison because there are no comparable statistics at the level of the microregions across the union. we must now examine whether, as these are predominantly rural areas, it is right to link rural depopulation to agricultural policy. would it not be much more appropriate to try to solve the rural problem within the framework of cohesion policy, with cohesion instruments, and this would only be complemented by what agricultural policy can add to this? i would like to thank the shadow rapporteurs for the very constructive work they have done in preparing this report. at the same time, i would like to point out that after several weeks of negotiations, after i had accepted the report of the people's party, which made a very substantial change in one of the most important points, the people's party voted against its own point, and after that i have to ask myself what do we want? are we willing to say that there is a problem and we don't want to achieve anything else, or are we willing to face the problem and propose that we change our policy and focus more on where we can solve the problem? thank you very much for your attention.

thank you very much, mr. president. the new waste directive contains compromises and can therefore be attacked from several angles. i understand the concern of the environmentally conscious citizens who have petitioned me to reject the bill. i must also say that a number of my individual amendments have been incorporated into the text, and they were born in a progressive spirit in cooperation with the green organisations. the main merit of the directive is the adoption of a waste hierarchy. prevention comes first, then recycling in various forms, and then waste, which should be avoided if possible, but at least reduced. i voted for the directive because i believe in the hierarchy. we hope to improve the law in the future.

functioning of the market. in many member states today, consumers still compete for service providers, rather than the other way around. in hungary, for example, liberalization now means higher prices madam president, officer! we have made a huge step in one year, because a common energy policy can be a huge step for the whole union. an energy policy that serves the strategic interests of the eu member states, the rights and protection of europe's citizens, the competitiveness and growth of the european economy and the protection of our environment. the five proposals envisage a market integration in which, in addition to the separation and transparency of energy production, transmission and distribution tasks, the fundamental rights and protection of energy consumers are given greater emphasis than ever before in the transformation of the operation of energy companies. the clean competition of the market, the promotion of crossborder energy trade and the quality of consumer the rapporteur. - mr president, madam president, i have to go. i would like to start with what the vice-president of the commission started with, namely that this is indeed a bilateral matter. on the one hand, it is a very technical question - but i would add that it is our job to solve the technical problems and the related tasks, and we must understand them - and on the other hand, it is a new opportunity for citizens, because, through their freely elected representatives, who are directly elected, the new procedure, the new control procedure, actually gives them for the first time the real opportunity to control the implementing provisions and the normative provisions of the european commission. you've heard a lot of stories, mostly from eurosceptic circles, about the european commission and the european bureaucracy wanting to regulate a lot of little, detailed things. from the size of an egg to the color of a pepper, we hear all kinds of horror stories. well, we don't know how true that is, of course. in any case, the myths surrounding the brussels bureaucracy are still alive today. one reason for the persistence of this myth is that the european parliament has not yet had a genuine power of control over the commission's delegated acts and normative acts. this current process, which will be completed - at least in part - by the two votes tomorrow, will enable parliament to exercise this control, and these normative acts will now be subject to political control, and to put it in the usual terms of the democratic deficit, i must say that this is a significant step. otherwise, without a treaty amendment, it's a significant step towards eliminating the democratic deficit. i have always been on it, and so has parliament, since the agreement between the european commission and parliament, which is the basis for this change, was signed in 2006. i have always been in favour of not having to wait for the treaty of lisbon to extend this power of attorney to parliament. we do not have to wait, because the agreement between the two institutions and the proposal put forward will remedy these problems to a considerable extent. moreover, today no one will be able to say when the treaty of lisbon will actually enter into force. parliament needs these licences. and perhaps a warning to ourselves, members of parliament, because this is a huge task: the council has been doing this for many years through the comitology committees. this is a completely new task for parliament and we have a lot of work to do to make sure that we can really exercise this new, opening licence. and it is not just that we can exercise these powers as a parliament, but that we are now obliged to do so, because the citizens can hold us, as elected representatives, accountable for the way in which we have handled these matters. i would like to thank the commission very much for putting forward these proposals, and i would like to point out that only 12 of the 30 or 46 dossiers on the so-called 'third list' have been submitted, and we would ask it to submit the remaining 34 dossiers as soon as possible so that we can discuss them. we have successfully concluded negotiations with the council on packages one and three. i have great confidence in him and the council's commitment to the company to continue this process and hopefully by the end of this year we can solve, we can complete this process. this will of course require further cooperation between all the institutions. this is also important because in this case both the commission and the council are giving up important constitutional powers in favour of parliament, so i would say that it is very honourable and we thank you for giving us these rights, but i am convinced that you have done this in the interests of a better functioning, more transparent and more democratic union, in order to eliminate excessive bureaucratic procedures in the democracy of the european union. i thank the institutions for their cooperation and i thank all the committees for the rapid procedure by which parliament has resolved this matter, at least as regards the first and third packages. thank you for your attention.
the rapporteur. - mr president, this is the honorable house. in fact, i have little choice but to thank my colleagues on the legal affairs committee and on the other committees for their close cooperation and all the staff who have helped me with this very complex and difficult matter. i am very pleased that we have been able to reach agreement with the council on this matter and i hope that we can conclude this process at first reading. and i would also like to express my hope that this process will continue at the same pace and pace as the second and fourth packages - the fourth omnibus package is perhaps a little more difficult and more convulsive - but negotiations have already begun on this, and i am very confident that there is a good chance that by the end of this year, or even earlier, we will be able to conclude this process and officially start this process. the treaty of lisbon, by the way, introduces this system in the delegated acts itself, but it would have been wrong to waste two years without the comitology procedure. i am convinced that parliament needs this learning process, to get its bearings on these things. one of the speakers mentioned that it is important that we ourselves can distinguish between the relevant and the irrelevant issues, indeed it is the job of legislators to be able to focus on the relevant issues, therefore certain powers should be delegated to the executive, to the commission, but we should keep in mind the possibility and the right to control these implementing acts. the comments made were incorporated into the proposal. i thank you very much for your attention and i wish us, the commission and the council every success in the remaining tasks, because there are now some 60 pieces of legislation, the vast majority of which are still to be dealt with and hopefully will be dealt with in the second half of the year. thank you very much for your attention. thank you so much for the audition.

the rapporteur. - thank you very much. madam president, i have to ask you to leave. officer, please! my colleagues! we are becoming more and more dependent on transportation in our daily lives. the european economy cannot do without efficient road transport either. but the growing traffic is raising serious social problems. it causes traffic jams in roads and urban areas, serious environmental and public health problems, energy waste and, above all, accidents. some shocking figures to summarise: road transport emits 835 million tonnes of co2 a year; 40 000 people die on the roads every year; the cost of congestion in the union is estimated at eur 50 billion a year, equivalent to 0.5% of community gdp, and this figure could reach 1% of eu gdp by 2010. but the good news is that these problems can be alleviated. electronic stability control could save around 4000 lives a year, if the ecall could be fully deployed in europe, reduce road injuries and deaths by up to 5-15% a year, which would mean more than 2500 lives a year and savings of 22 billion euros. with the right software and real-time information, traffic jams in transportation hubs could be reduced by 40%. the above few figures show that the benefits of intelligent vehicle systems are undeniable, but they are less widespread, while in the united states, for example, electronic stability control will be mandatory in all new cars from 2012. the main reasons for this are the high cost of equipment, the lack of adequate information and the lack of intelligent infrastructure. but we must ask ourselves: can european citizens be denied the exercise of their rights? because i believe that each of them, and each of us, has the right to safe, sustainable and efficient mobility. the above problems are european in nature because of increased and constantly growing mobility, and if mobility is not optimal or safe, then we need to respond at european level. market barriers should be removed, demand for intelligent communication technologies should be stimulated and a european-wide interoperable intelligent infrastructure should be established. the european achievements in research and development in information and communication technologies should be maintained or improved, in particular with regard to the appropriate driver-driver interface. this is essential for the european economy, society and industry, as japanese and us industries are very serious competitors (to name a few). this has been recognised by european decision-makers and the smart car has been identified as one of the flagship initiatives of the e-2010 initiative. overall, therefore, i welcome the european commission's communication and i support the efforts of the parties concerned to ensure the appropriate deployment of intelligent information and safe technologies in vehicles. however, we must recognise that we are still at the beginning of the process and that we are currently informed about the developments that have taken place since the launch of the 'intelligent car' initiative. we've got a lot of work to do, and we're not doing badly. at the same time, i look forward to the next progress reports and am confident that i will contribute, with my modest means, to the goal of spreading life-saving, environmental and traffic-optimising systems as widely as possible. last but not least, i would like to take this opportunity to thank all those who have helped me with my work: the directorate-general for information society and media of the european commission, the secretariat of the parliament's committee on transport and tourism, my dear colleagues in the house and those who cannot be here for comments, amendments, professional organisations for the many useful advice, invitations and explanations. thank you so much for your cooperation.
mr. president, i have to go. i support the report by the rapporteur, ms gurmai. mr. vice president, i have to go. the time has come to open a new era in the history of european transport by implementing intelligent transport. we know that if we want to respond properly to climate change, one of the most important tools we have, one of the most important weapons we have, is to make intelligent transportation a reality. just two years ago, here in the european parliament's plenary, i said that today, i clearly see the most important challenge facing transport as being to succeed in the deployment of intelligent transport systems in europe. then, a year later, i presented the european parliament's review of the white paper on european transport policy here in the house of lords, and then parliament clearly supported smart transport, just as it does now, as a top priority of european transport policy. then why are we moving so slowly? usually, the technology is ready. for example, the smart car, ict-based urban traffic management systems, egnos from 2009, galileo from 2013. one of europe's industrial strengths is the automotive sector, where we are indeed successful in global competition, not least thanks to the highly innovative smes associated with it. and now the developments are sweeping across western and eastern europe. in budapest, for example, two very important european centres of knowledge operate alongside the technical university. so the industry committed itself. now it is up to the community side, the member states in particular, to act. the commission notes that the commission has not provided any evidence that the measure is selective. of course, new technologies are not easy to introduce, but we can support this through, for example, well-chosen public procurement. we have a lot of tools. we ask the commission to develop an appropriate and full cross-sectoral strategy for the implementation of intelligent transport. europeans waste precious minutes, half an hour, on the roads. meanwhile, motor vehicles pollute the air unnecessarily, while personal mobility is endangered, partly because the cost of fuel for vehicles used in this way is becoming unaffordable. one of the key pillars of the solution is intelligent transport. the european citizens are sensitive to all solutions, and today all solutions, to the problems outlined here. let's move forward.

elements that could correct existing legislation. i cannot stress enough in my speeches how important it is to declare school segregation as discrimination in the legislation that this particular race directive 2000/43 has set out. the directive merely states that discrimination in schools between groups of children of different racial or ethnic origin is prohibited. this policy does not say that segregation, educational segregation is discrimination. we have a lot of evidence of this, not only from ngos, not only from the european commission and not only from various european parliament resolutions, but also from hungarian legislation, which has already proven that this is discrimination. considering this important aspect in the development of the new horizontal directive and the recommendations of the so-called 'roma decade programme' for the five member states as the development of a european roma strategy would be very important and effective for the integration of roma in i welcome the fact that, after the reach regulation and the regulation of pesticides, we are now in line to regulate the use of food additives, a chemical that is so often condemned. the european public is increasingly concerned about these substances, which are suspect to consumers, as a result of the growing scandals. of the approximately 2600 different additives with e-numbers, the average person consumes about 3 kg per year without being aware of their harmful effects. a significant proportion of the additives have already been the subject of more or less well-founded allegations. additives give consumers a false impression of adding vibrant and vibrant colours to food that do not exist in nature. in addition to the health risks, they cause psychological harm to children by habituating them to unnatural foods, when it is the return to natural foods that should be promoted among young and old alike. i congratulate åsa westlund on her report, which has succeeded in ensuring that additives harmful to human health and the environment cannot be used in the future under the new legislation. it is important that hazardous substances such as azo dyes, which cause hyperactivity in children, should at least be labelled on foodstuffs, although i agree with my other colleagues that we should have adopted a ban. i regret, however, that despite parliament's proposal, additives containing gmos do not have to be strictly labelled. the majority of the european population does not want to eat food containing gmos and surveys show that at least 90% of them would expect to know whether a given food contains gmos or not. to such an extent, we cannot neglect the healthy concern of our citizens, the right to information. i have also made a statement with some of my colleagues protesting the commission's intention to smuggle in more gmos to europe. we must protect our right to natural and healthy food from everyone.
in writing. – i would like first of all to congratulate the rapporteurs on these reports on food additives, enzymes and flavouring. it is essential that our parliament pays the utmost attention to these regulations as they have a huge impact on public health and we ought therefore to ensure that the amendments we supported on first reading are included in the final text. protecting consumers’ interests and ensuring food security must remain here our first priorities. we should – in line respect to the precautionary principle – avoid the use of certain compounds for which we lack reliable data and independent scientific assessment on their consequences on consumers’ health. we ought indeed to ensure quality and safe food for our citizens and should therefore develop efficient methodologies to list and monitor the effects on consumers’ health of potentially dangerous compounds. moreover, we have to increase transparency in the production, the labelling and the authorisation procedures as well as harmonising regulations on additives, enzymes and flavouring to set up a coherent regulatory package ensuring better consumer protection and public health. while ensuring consumer’s protection the real challenge is to actually regain consumers’ confidence and ensure a quality food production for their benefit.

on behalf of the ppe-de group. mr president, once again we have heard voices saying that this proposal is also about restricting the activities of certain members of parliament. i would like to call the attention of the eurosceptic members to the fact that before making such a claim, they should read the proposal. this report, this change in the rules, should have been done a long time ago, rest is spent on other activities. in fact, this report enables parliament to do what the treaties empower it to do. we can therefore concentrate on legislation, and this does not mean that we should disregard the own-initiative reports, which also record very important points, but we must know that their impact is substantially less than what we can achieve through legislation. when we create thank you for your time, mr. president. my fellow members of the house of representatives! the baltic sea gas pipeline is one of the most controversial issues in the emerging common energy policy. many are concerned that the pipeline will not increase the continent's security of supply, but our vulnerability to russia. it is not known whether the prices will be more affordable for the consumers concerned or whether they will skyrocket as a result of the gigantic investment. and finally, there is no clear answer to the questions on the agenda today: what are the environmental consequences of the nord stream project? , in writing. addressing the serious challenges of climate change, strengthening and accelerating action on the issue is an unavoidable task. it is essential to reduce greenhouse gas emissions, including from aviation, and to set an accurate emission ceiling for the period after 2012. the reduction of greenhouse gases will only be achieved if the emissions trading system is tightened. there is a need for pragmatic use of auction revenues (such as development and use of green technology), especially in sectors supported by the european union, and in this context it is worth considering the creation of a separate financial fund. the early completion of the galileo, ses and ets programmes as a whole would also contribute to fuel efficiency. climate change is a global phenomenon. all policy instruments and negotiating means should be used to involve all the world's major greenhouse gas emitters in the global reduction of pollution.

in writing. enlargement is one of the eu's most successful policies and perhaps the most effective foreign policy tool ever known. all the enlargements so far have strengthened the union, stabilised it and generally brought the new countries closer together. the four years since the 2004 enlargement have been a time of unfounded fears and misinformation: enlargement has been an overwhelming success, from which both old and new have benefited immensely. unfortunately, some politicians, either deliberately or out of sheer stupidity, deny the success of enlargement and mislead citizens in the old member states. it is therefore of the utmost importance that we make the benefits of enlargement known to society. the rejection of the lisbon treaty by ireland is indeed a serious obstacle to further enlargement of the union. i remain confident that the eu will soon find a solution to save the lisbon treaty. however, croatia's accession cannot be held hostage until then: it is legally possible for croatia to join without lisbon coming into force. depending on the progress of the accession negotiations, croatia could therefore be admitted as early as the end of 2009 or the beginning of 2010. the relationship between the enlargement strategy and the european neighbourhood policy is a complex issue. i am in principle in favour of our european neighbours, who do not yet have a membership perspective, being able to move from one category to another in accordance with their measurable objective performance. at the same time, it is important that the union can maintain its geopolitical freedom of movement and, given its capacity for integration, decide in individual cases what perspective it offers its partners.
the eu's history to date has been one of continuous enlargement, and this enlargement is also the most visible evidence of the success and attractiveness of the european union. yet, there is a growing sense in the public of a kind of expansion fatigue, expansion apathy. this encourages all of us to be realistic about enlargement. but realism is not skepticism. it cannot mean stopping the enlargement process, much less formulating new, unenforceable recruitment conditions or resigning from commitments already made, since that would also jeopardise our credibility. after the irish referendum, it is in our fundamental, basic interest to prove that the european union continues to work and that enlargement remains an important and existing objective in the european union. this is in the interest of the member states as much as the applicant countries. thank you, sir.
in writing. – the process of enlargement has been an integral part of the development of the eu over the last 50 years. from the six founding members, the eu has enlarged to 27 member states and represents more than 450 million people the european union is more stable, more secure and has a bigger voice on international issues than before. we have experienced through the years that enlargement has been at the core of the success and development of the eu. enlargement has enabled the peaceful reunification of europe across the cold war divide. we have to agree that development of the eu has gone hand in hand with enlargement. but what is the current attitude towards enlargement inside our society? attitudes towards further enlargement are unenthusiastic, mostly influenced by non-enlargement-related issues and partly related to the lack of information about enlargement. i am one of those who are convinced that the prospect of eventual eu membership is indispensable to driving further political reform and pushing for democracy. the fear is that if the prospect of eu membership is taken off the political agenda, the balkans may slip back into instability.
in writing. mr president! in the context of the enlargement strategy debate, i would like to draw attention to the important aspects affecting our immediate neighbours, the western balkans and ukraine. the prospect of accession, and in particular the obligation to meet the copenhagen criteria, has been a tremendous driving force in modernising the candidate countries and in building the rule of law. this is the kind of momentum that the western balkans and ukraine need. if we take away the prospect of accession from our immediate neighbours, who share european traditions, history, we lose the impetus that can bring about the full rule of law in these countries. the phenomena and legislation that are immediately apparent are the anti-minority education decrees that are causing outrage in ukraine, the closure of mother-tongue schools, the loss of language and assimilation. the moment that the system of requirements is relaxed, or the goal that is worth fulfilling is removed, these systems are removed from what we call the rule of law. that's why we have a great responsibility. it is important to ensure that the criteria are met in practice, not just on paper. we need consistency in our neighbours' preparations and consistency in the union's promises and demands. our consistent behavior builds trust with our constituents and our neighbors alike.

in writing. creativity and innovation play a crucial role in europe's ability to respond effectively to the challenges and opportunities of globalisation. the more efficient use of knowledge and the development of innovation are the lifeblood of the european economy and therefore need to be given special attention. the european year for creativity and innovation aims to raise awareness of this. the dynamism of the european economy depends to a large extent on its capacity to innovate. europe needs to increase its capacity for creativity and innovation, for both social and economic reasons. i therefore believe it is important that the european year should also be about practical measures to promote the development of innovation. the results of creativity and innovation should also be widely recognised. there is therefore a particular need to launch information and promotion campaigns, to organise events at european, national, regional and local level, to formulate key messages and to present good practices. cluster networks, the creation of knowledge triangles and the advancement of different forms of education, recognised as drivers of innovation, should be promoted. in order to foster innovation, member states should focus on improvements in innovation support services, in particular for the purpose of technology transfer, and on the creation of innovation hubs and networks by connecting universities and businesses, knowledge transfer, better access to finance.
the rapporteur. − it is a longstanding human experience that decision-making powers, especially those in executive positions, should not be left without parliamentary scrutiny. today, especially in the run-up to the lisbon treaty, we have heard a great deal about the so-called democratic deficit in the european union, and we have heard a great deal about the bureaucracy in brussels. interestingly, the very people who would benefit most from this change are not the ones who support the lisbon treaty today, the very people who would be convinced that the lisbon treaty actually provides more effective control over the executive, the european commission and the so-called european bureaucracy. i would like to make this point to all those who think that the treaty of lisbon is not necessary. i am convinced that by giving the european parliament, directly elected by the citizens, greater powers over the european commission, we are all serving this purpose. in particular, as the parliament and the council, the legislative powers, delegate more and more powers to the executive, the european commission is increasingly entrusted with the task of drawing up the detailed rules. there are many cases where immediate action is needed, where the complex european mechanism, the legislative mechanism, the council and parliament cannot come together and there is a need for a quick reaction, and in this case there is no other option than to empower the commission to act quickly and effectively in these cases. it's happening now. these two reports concern the fact that, under the agreement concluded between the european parliament and the commission in 2006, the powers and authority of the european parliament also extend to the scrutiny of acts of the commission in its executive or legislative capacity. until now, only one legislator, the council, has had the opportunity to do so. the european commission has put forward four packages, two of which were adopted at our summer session, and now the third, my own-initiative report, is about the principles on which this process should be based and how this control should be recorded. at the same time, i would like to make it clear that a good compromise was reached in the negotiations, but i would like to make it absolutely clear that this is not a precedent for the current comitology procedure or for the future. this audit mandate will be given to parliament regardless of the treaty of lisbon, which means that the treaty of lisbon, which provides for similar mandates, has come a little further and we are already implementing them in this context. this is a huge task for parliament, and parliament must provide the necessary means for this, which is why i have also acted in the budgetary field to ensure that parliament, apart from the bureaucracies, has the resources it needs to carry out this new task to the full. in other words, parliament must also be prepared to fulfil this task in full, and i believe that by doing so, by taking this step, the european union will also become more democratic, more transparent, more transparent, that is, we will take a significant step towards the control of the executive power by the legislative power, and thus by the sovereignty of the people in the european institutions. thank you for your attention.
in writing. – european roma have been subjected to low-quality education in the form of segregated education and gypsy-only classrooms, not only in the post-communist countries but in europe as a whole. roma children encounter difficulties in receiving quality education in new and old member states alike. geographical segregation of roma is a major obstacle to equality in education. the living conditions of roma make it difficult to take the steps necessary in the long battle to eliminate gypsy-only classrooms. making progress on this fight depends on highly-qualified teachers from roma and non-roma backgrounds to ensure the essential development of roma children and to change the status quo. providing highly-qualified teachers to give roma children the challenges found in mainstream schools is of huge relevance to the education future teachers receive in teacher-training colleges. if we are to expect results from our children we must provide our teachers with exemplary training. the knowledge about progressive teachings in multiculturally diverse classrooms must be a main focus in future teacher training. the goal of equalising the quality of education between schools, stopping early drop-outs, and making sure roma children make it to university and that they are prepared to face life’s challenges is not one taken lightly but one we must endeavour to fight for.
in writing. the financial crisis that started in america last year, triggered by unregulated, speculative financial processes, has not only subsided as expected, but has already shocked the whole world, including europe. the current crisis requires a long-term paradigm shift in both areas from european decision-makers in order not only to reduce the risk of a financial crisis in the future, but also to promote stable economic growth. the us developments have shown that the market alone is not able to cope with such and similar crises. the commission is therefore proposing to set up as soon as possible the central european financial supervisor, which was proposed by hungary last year and has since been endorsed by the french presidency. this would ensure, among other things, that such risky speculative transactions are placed in controllable and predictable conditions in the banking and financial system. this would be a prerequisite for europe to gradually take over the role of a shaken america in the financial world. in order to put the european economy, which is also suffering from the current crisis, on the desired growth path as soon as possible, the financial and banking world would need to finally put the financing of the real economy ahead of risky speculative transactions. one prerequisite is that the european central bank should not simply concentrate on fighting inflation as it has done in the past, but should also promote the recovery of the real economy through more favourable interest rates.
the only way to stop climate change is to stop deforestation. our efforts to protect the environment in europe will be ineffective if we tolerate environmental degradation in other parts of the world. the liberalization of international trade, globalization, is driving environmental destruction across continents. the wto rules must be supplemented by strict environmental standards, otherwise further liberalisation will lead to further environmental destruction. it is not enough to ban the illegal timber trade, we must also ban the import of products made from illegally harvested timber, furniture. do you really think cheap chinese furniture imports have nothing to do with deforestation? this report is a step in the right direction, but more radical measures are needed because the state of our planet demands it. until stricter rules are in place, we need to call on large international furniture retailers, such as ikea, to apply transparent self-restriction, not to trade in furniture made from illegal wood. and finally, deforestation is not only driven by the timber trade, the furniture trade, but also by rising agricultural prices, by rising demand for biofuels. if we allow them to trade in biofuels that are produced by deforestation, we need to know that every time we put that fuel in our cars, we are contributing to the destruction of a few square meters of primary forest.
in writing. there is no longer any doubt that deforestation and forest degradation have an impact on climate change and biodiversity. to be precise, global deforestation now affects 13 million hectares a year and is the third largest source of greenhouse gas emissions. illegal logging causes erosion, undermines the livelihoods of local communities and costs timber-producing countries between eur 10 and 15 billion a year. of course, i welcome the establishment of an international agreement on tropical timber, but that is not even close to achieving the goal. we will only get there if we succeed in getting a more comprehensive approach to temperate forests, at least in the european union. an approach that ensures that timber products are produced in a fair manner and that the entire supply chain can be traced. only such an agreement could make a significant contribution to the protection of forests and the sustainable use of timber. i have no illusions, of course, especially since this spring and summer, when i wrote a written declaration on this subject with several of my colleagues, only a quarter of the members signed it. i hope that the tropical forests will eventually turn their attention to us, to europe. perhaps, thanks to an agreement on tropical forests, the commission will come up with legislation requiring that only legally harvested timber and timber products may be placed on the union market.
thank you for your time, mr. president. the financial crisis that began in america a year ago has not abated, contrary to expectations, but has struck the entire world, including the financial markets of europe and, through them, the economy, in ever-increasing waves. the central bank has to pump huge amounts of money into the money markets in order to ensure their survival. however, in order to remedy the economic and social impact of the financial crisis, the commission needs to make progress in two areas. first, a common european financial supervisory body should be set up as soon as possible to prevent such speculative risks, thereby ensuring that europe gradually takes over the role of the troubled us money and capital markets. on the other hand, it should also ensure that the european financial sector concentrates on financing the real economy, which has been neglected so far, instead of speculative transactions. this would, of course, require the european central bank to prioritise economic growth and job creation in addition to keeping inflation in check. thank you very much.

mr president, i would like to speak about only one aspect of energy prices, namely the price of district heating. in hungary, hundreds of thousands of households use district heating – mostly families with modest incomes – and its price is a crucial social issue. however, due to the run-down infrastructure and for other technical reasons, district heating is far more expensive than individual heating. this contradicts common sense and the basic interests of the environment. that is why, whenever i can, i urge the commission and the council to provide financial support to improve district-heating systems in order to make it socially acceptable and environmentally efficient. as long as district heating is more expensive in some member states than individual heating, it is nonsense to speak about energy efficiency.
in writing. in recent months, we have seen unprecedented increases in energy prices. the increase in energy-related costs could have a very negative impact on europe's competitiveness. however, it is precisely the most vulnerable in society that are most affected. the problem is compounded by the fact that some countries subordinate the resources at their disposal to political goals. as a result, rising energy prices have become one of the most sensitive policy issues. while no member state can avoid the impact of price increases, some countries are particularly vulnerable. due to the inaction and misguided government policies, hungarian consumers were forced to endure four times the increase in gas prices in 2008 alone. that is why the rise in energy prices has become one of the most important sources of social discontent in hungary. to keep prices affordable, concerted and consistent action is needed. every effort should be made to ensure that the system of long-term international contracts governing energy supply is transparent. at the same time, measures to promote competition and improve energy efficiency should be strengthened at both community and member state level. the european union must take substantive steps to achieve this, in particular by developing alternative energy supply routes. we also consider it appropriate to coordinate measures to protect the most vulnerable. we believe that the increase in electricity prices cannot be accompanied by a deepening of social disparities.

in writing. − ‘community media’ often plays an important role in local communities. it is an influential source of information amongst the local media; sometimes even the sole voice of local communities. that is why the european union should focus more on these media, especially after the failure of the lisbon treaty, as they may serve as an efficient means to pass on eu related information to citizens. as rapporteur for the report on the active dialogue with citizens on europe, i fully support any kind of communication tool which may help to bring closer the eu to the citizens. nevertheless, i am convinced that an essential precondition for community media as well as any other local media financed, even partially, from public funds, is to be independent not only from national but also from local power. i know that community media, especially their financing, should be a primary concern of member states, due to their various forms and local specialities. where we may help at european level is in giving more visibility to the issue. this report is the first step in that direction.

in writing. the arctic has become a symbol of climate change in recent years. the region is also at risk of becoming a hotbed of international conflict because of its untapped resources. the region's valuation is also indicated by the fact that there is a state that has given a clear signal of claim to the territory. it is not the role of the european parliament to get involved in geographical debates about the integration of the region. but i want to draw your attention to a few factors. first, if there are unresolved issues, they can only be resolved through legal and diplomatic means, not by flags. we believe that the current international order, while far from perfect, can provide a framework for resolving the issue through diplomatic means. on the other hand, given the growing demand in europe, the untapped energy resources in the north pole could contribute to ensuring european supply. however, it should be pointed out that the extraction must not affect the biological balance of the area: environmental considerations must be taken into account in all circumstances. i believe that the current international law does not yet provide a satisfactory guarantee of this. it is important to note that the exploitation of the arctic is expected to play only an ancillary role in europe's energy supply. these sources should therefore not replace measures to promote the use of renewable energy sources and improve energy efficiency.
in writing. according to the 2007 eurobarometer survey, 5% of eu citizens in working age, or 19.7 million of the eu's 392.9 million inhabitants, said they had worked illegally. in some member states, undeclared work accounts for 20% or more of gdp. undeclared work is a persistent phenomenon with negative economic effects on the european labour market, a vicious circle from which it is very difficult for workers and employers to break out. the sectors most affected are agriculture, construction, households, accommodation and catering, where uncertainty and unfavourable wage conditions are common. the fight against undeclared work and the modernisation of labour laws and labour standards cannot therefore be confined to the level of mere theory, but requires concerted action by the member states. i suggest that the commission develop a european union strategy to combat undeclared work and take a greater part in the work of labour and social inspectorates. member states should continue to reform tax and social security systems, thereby reducing the tax burden on labour. in order to enable households to purchase services at lower prices, it would be proposed to introduce union service vouchers, which would also ensure the payment of social security contributions and taxes. however, in order to combat undeclared work, it is essential that member states open their labour markets to each other's workers, since their partial restrictions contribute to the creation of territorial disparities and are contrary to the principles of the union and the european spirit.
in writing. – the necessity of eradicating child poverty is felt to be an important issue within the european union. roma children especially are the most deprived minority, lacking assistance in the areas of housing, employment, education and healthcare. the combination of these factors results in a dire situation where romani children have no future and where roma and romani children are socially excluded, especially in cases where romani children grow up in extreme poverty. this type of environment denies romani children the chance to exercise their existing rights which they deserve. roma children who grow up in poverty lack the opportunities given freely to the majority. social exclusion and child poverty must be taken into consideration during eu and member states’ policy-making, especially in areas relating to active social inclusion. current data show about 50% of roma are illiterate or semi-illiterate; 30% of children aged 3-10 are suffering from serious illness such as tb; the life expectancy of roma is 10 years lower than the majority. the level of social exclusion that roma encounter in childhood is compounded as they grow into adults. in most european cities 90% of roma are unemployed and have little hope of finding employment in the near future; this vicious circle has to be broken for the social cohesion of the eu.
